.

Lemma 15
.

Let
S
be a sequence of shape descriptions from the feasible sets of
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{1}}
,
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{2}}
,
\dots
,
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
. We can decide whether
S
is a shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and compute the corresponding shape descriptions of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
in
\mathcal{O}(r^{3})
time, where
r
is the size of
S
. Furthermore there are
\mathcal{O}(r^{2})
computed shape descriptions of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
.

Proof.

Assume first that
S
is reduced. We want to use
UP0
-
UP3
in order to compute all possible corresponding shape descriptions of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Let
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
be an upward planar embedding of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
with shape sequence
S
. Let
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
be a component of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
its
uv
-external upward embedding induced by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
and let
s_{i}=\langle\tau_{l}(s_{i}),\allowbreak\tau_{r}(s_{i}),\allowbreak\lambda_{u}(s_{i}),\allowbreak\lambda_{v}(s_{i}),\allowbreak\rho_{l}(u,s_{i}),\allowbreak\rho_{r}(u,s_{i}),\allowbreak\rho_{l}(v,s_{i}),\allowbreak\rho_{r}(v,s_{i})\rangle
be its shape description in
S
.
For all faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
,
UP3
gives
\tau_{r}(s_{i})+\tau_{l}(s_{i+1})+\lambda(\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i}))+\lambda(\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=-2
holds if
i\neq 0
, and
\tau_{r}(s_{k^{\prime}})+\tau_{l}(s_{1})+\lambda(\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{0}))+\lambda(\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{0}))=2
for the outer face
f^{\prime}_{0}
. For the labels at the angles
\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i})
and
\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i})
, we have that
\lambda(\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=0
(i.e.
\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{i})
is a flat angle) if and only if
\rho_{r}(w,s_{i})\neq\rho_{l}(w,s_{i+1})
, where
w
is a pole of
\mu
. Hence the label of
\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{i})
is not determined only if it is a switch angle and its label can be either
-1
or
1
. Note that by
UP2
there is exactly one angle with label
1
. Then, if
u
is a switch vertex, the possible labelings of all angles
\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i})
are
r+1
; namely there are
r
faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
that can have the unique label
1
, or none of them has label
1
. Similarly if
v
is a switch vertex, the possible labelings of all angles
\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i})
are
r+1
. This implies that there exist in total at most
(r+1)^{2}
different labelings for the angles at
u
and
v
inside all faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. For each labeling, we want to test whether
UP3
holds for all faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. This can be done in constant time for each face, and thus in
\mathcal{O}(r)
time for all faces. Still we need to guarantee that
UP1
and
UP2
also hold.

Assume that pole
w
is a switch vertex of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Note that if the shape description for a component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
has label at pole
w
equal to
-1
, that is
\lambda(w,\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}})=-1
, then the large angle of
w
is inside an interior face of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
. Then
\lambda(\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=-1
must hold for all faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
, and
\lambda(w,\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}})=1
for all components
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}}
different from
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
. Otherwise,
\lambda(w,\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}})=1
holds for all components
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
, and the large angle of
w
must be inside a face
f^{\prime}_{i}
of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
, that is
\lambda(\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=1
and
\lambda(\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{j}))=-1
for all other faces
f^{\prime}_{j}
. Note that all shape descriptions used for the components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
belong to their feasible sets. Then all angles of
w
in the interior of all components (except possibly for one angle in the interior of one
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
) have label
-1
. This implies that the above restrictions are sufficient to test whether
UP1
holds for
w
, and this can be done in
\mathcal{O}(r)
time for each labeling.
Now, assume that pole
w
is a non-switch vertex of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Note that if the shape description for a component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
has label at pole
w
equal to
-1
, that is
\lambda(w,\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}})=-1
, then both flat angles of
u
are inside

\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
, while if the label equals
0
that is
\lambda(w,\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}})=0
, then one flat angle of
u
is inside
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
. In the first case, we have that
\lambda(\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=-1
must hold for all faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
, and
\lambda(w,\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}})=1
for all components
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}}
different from
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
. In the second case,
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
is the unique special component of pole
w
, and
\lambda(w,\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}})=1
must hold for all other components
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}}
, while
\lambda(\alpha_{w}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=1
must hold for all faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
, except for one that must have label
0
. In both cases, since all shape descriptions used for the components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
belong to their feasible sets, the switch angles of
w
in the interior of all components have label
-1
. Hence the above restrictions are sufficient to test whether
UP2
holds for
w
, and this can be done in
\mathcal{O}(r)
time for each labeling.

Overall, there exist
\mathcal{O}(r^{2})
possible labelings for the angles
\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i})
and
\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i})
of
u
and
v
respectively inside all faces
f^{\prime}_{i}
of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Testing whether
UP1
-
UP3
holds for each of them requires
\mathcal{O}(r)
time. If this is the case, by Theorem
1

G^{\prime}_{\mu}
is upward planar and the shape description of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
can be computed in constant time from the first element of
S
, its last element, and the labels at
u
and
v
chosen for the outer face
f^{\prime}_{0}
. Thus, the overall complexity of deciding whether a sequence
S
is a shape sequence for
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
is
\mathcal{O}(r^{3})
. In the same time all
\mathcal{O}(r^{2})
shape descriptions for
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
that correspond to
S
are computed.

Assume now that
S
is not reduced. We describe how this case can be brought down to the previous one. Let
s\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}
be a maximal subsequence of
S
. Let
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}}
,
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j+1}}
,
\dots
,
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j+\ell}}
be the components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
that correspond to the subsequence
s\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}
and let
G^{+}_{\mu}
be the subgraph of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
consisting of these components. Let
S^{-}
be the sequence obtained from
S
by replacing
s\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}
with
s
and let
G^{-}_{\mu}
the graph obtained from
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
by replacing
G^{+}_{\mu}
with
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}}
.

First we check under which conditions
s\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}
is a shape sequence for
G^{+}_{\mu}
. Let
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
and
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i+1}}
be two consecutive components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
that both have shape description
s
in
S
. The internal face
f^{\prime}_{i}
of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
is defined by the right outer path of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
and the left outer path of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i+1}}
. Let
\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i})
and
\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i})
be the angles at
u
and
v
respectively inside
f^{\prime}_{i}
. If
S
is a shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
,
UP3
must be satisfied for
f^{\prime}_{i}
, that is
\tau_{r}(s)+\tau_{l}(s)+\lambda(\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i}))+\lambda(\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=-2
. Recall that by Lemma
9
the right-turn-number of
s
satisfies
\tau_{r}(s)=-\tau_{l}(s)+h
with
h=0,1,2,3,4
. Also the labels
\lambda(\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i}))
and
\lambda(\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i}))
take values in
\{-1,0,1\}
. The previous equation gives
h+\lambda(\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i}))+\lambda(\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=-2
which is only satisfied if
h=0
and
\lambda(\alpha_{u}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=\lambda(\alpha_{v}(f^{\prime}_{i}))=-1
. Hence the shape description
s=\langle\tau_{l}(s),\allowbreak\tau_{r}(s),\allowbreak\lambda_{u}(s),\allowbreak\lambda_{v}(s),\allowbreak\rho_{l}(u,s),\allowbreak\rho_{r}(u,s),\allowbreak\rho_{l}(v,s),\allowbreak\rho_{r}(v,s)\rangle
must satisfy the thin conditions presented in Section
3
.
Checking whether
s
satisfies the thin conditions can be done in constant time. Let
N_{1}
be the set
\{\nu^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\nu^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\}
of children of
\mu
,
N_{s}=\{\nu^{\prime}_{j},\dots,\nu^{\prime}_{j+\ell}\}
and
N_{2}=N_{1}\setminus N_{s}\cup\{\nu^{\prime}_{j}\}
. Note that
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and
G^{-}_{\mu}
are the graphs
G_{N_{1}}
and
G_{N_{2}}
of Lemma
11
. Also, an upward embedding
\mathcal{E}_{N_{1}}
of
G_{N_{1}}
with shape sequence
S
, and an upward embedding
\mathcal{E}_{N_{2}}
of
G_{N_{2}}
with shape sequence
S^{-}
, both respect
s
for the set
N_{s}
, and they form an equivalence pair. Hence, by Lemma
11
, if
s
satisfies the thin conditions, then every shape description for
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
that corresponds to
S
is also a shape description for
G^{-}_{\mu}
that corresponds to
S^{-}
and vice versa.

We try to replace every maximal subsequence
s\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}
of
S
with
s
. If this is not possible, that is
s
does not satisfy the thin conditions, then
S
is not a shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Otherwise, we have a subgraph
G^{-}_{\mu}
of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and a sequence
S^{-}
which is reduced. By using the procedure described at the beginning of the proof, we can compute the shape descriptions of
G^{-}_{\mu}
that corresponds to
S^{-}
in
\mathcal{O}(r^{3})
time.
∎

Let
S^{\prime}
be the shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Let
\nu
be a child of
G_{\mu}
that is not in the set
\{\nu^{\prime}_{1},\nu^{\prime}_{2}\dots,\nu^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\}
, let
s
be a shape description from the feasible set of
G_{\nu}
, and let
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
be the subgraph of
G_{\mu}
consisting of the union of
G_{\nu}
and
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. We say that
S^{\prime}

can be extended
with
s
to a shape sequence
S^{\prime\prime}
of
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
if
S^{\prime\prime}
is a shape sequence of
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
,
s
belongs to
S^{\prime\prime}
, and removing
s
from
S^{\prime\prime}
we obtain
S^{\prime}
. Notice that
s
can be either the first, or the last, or an intermediate element of
S^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma 16
.

Let
S^{\prime}
be a shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Given a shape description
s
from the feasible set of
G_{\nu}
, we can decide whether
S^{\prime}
can be extended with
s
to a shape sequence
S^{\prime\prime}
of
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
and compute the corresponding shape descriptions for
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
in
\mathcal{O}(r^{4})
time, where
r
is the size of
S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The shape description
s
can be placed either at the beginning, at the end or between two consecutive elements of
S^{\prime}
. In the first two cases,
S^{\prime\prime}=[s,S^{\prime}]
or
S^{\prime\prime}=[S^{\prime},s]
. The shape descriptions that correspond to
S^{\prime\prime}
can be computed using Lemma
15
in
\mathcal{O}(r^{3})
time each. For the last case, let
a
and
b
be the two consecutive elements of
S^{\prime}
where
s
is placed. If
a\neq b
there are at most
r-1
possible placements for
s
, that is,
r-1
different sequences
S^{\prime\prime}
. Note that if
s=a
or
s=b
, then
S^{\prime\prime}
might be the same as
S^{\prime}
. On the other hand, if
a=b
there are at most
r
such sequences
S^{\prime\prime}
. In fact, arguing similarly with the first part of the proof of Lemma
15
, if
s\neq a
the sequence
S^{\prime\prime}
is not a shape sequence of
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
and if
s=a
then
S^{\prime\prime}
is the same sequence with
S^{\prime}
. By Lemma
15
, we can test in
\mathcal{O}(r^{3})
time if each produced sequence
S^{\prime\prime}
is a shape sequence of
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
or not. The total require time is
\mathcal{O}(r^{4})
as claimed.
∎

Suppose that
G_{\mu}
is upward planar and consider an upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
of
G_{\mu}
. We first remove the special components of
u
and
v
and the normal components
G_{\nu}
whose
uv
-external upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}_{\nu}
induced by
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
has shape description with
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu},u)=-1
or
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu},v)=-1
.
Denote by
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
the subgraph of
G_{\mu}
obtained after this removal. We say that
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
is the
thin subgraph
of
G_{\mu}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
. Denote by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
the upward planar embedding of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}

Let
\nu^{\prime}_{1},\nu^{\prime}_{2}\dots,\nu^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
be the children of
\mu
corresponding to the remaining components, that is, normal components of both
u
and
v
where
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},u)=\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},v)=1
for
i=1,\cdots,k^{\prime}
. Assume that the pertinent graphs
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{1}}
,
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{2}}
,
\dots
,
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
appear in this order from left to right around
u
, such that the outer boundary of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
is formed by the left outer path of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{1}}
and the right outer path of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
.
We first present few technical lemmata that will be used later in this section.

Lemma 17
.

The thin subgraph of
G_{\mu}
with respect to an upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
contains
k^{\prime}
components of
G_{\mu}
, where
k-2\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
.

Proof.

As
G_{\mu}
has
k
components, the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
contains
k^{\prime}\leq k
components of
G_{\mu}
. In order to prove that
k^{\prime}\geq k-2
we argue that there are most two components of
G_{\mu}
that don’t belong to the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
.
Note that if component
G_{\nu_{i}}
does not belong to
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
, then either
G_{\nu_{i}}
is a special component of
u
or
v
, or it is a normal component for both poles
u
and
v
, with
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{i}},u)=-1
or
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{i}},v)=-1
. We say that
G_{\nu_{i}}
is
associated
with pole
w
if
G_{\nu_{i}}
is either a special component of
w
or it is a normal component for both poles with
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{i}},w)=-1
. Note that if
G_{\nu_{i}}
is a special component of
w
then
w
is a non-switch vertex of
G_{\mu}
. On the other hand, if it is a normal component of
w
with
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{i}},w)=-1
, then there is a large angle at
w
inside
\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{i}}
and
w
is a switch vertex of
G_{\mu}
. Assume for a contradiction that there exist at least three components that don’t belong to the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Then at least two of them are associated with the same pole, say
w
. If
w
is a non-switch vertex, then both components are special components of
w
; contradiction, since there is at most one special component of
w
. Hence
w
is a switch vertex and both components are normal components with a large angle at
w
in their interior; contradiction to
UP2
, since, in any upward planar embedding, each switch vertex has exactly one large angle.
∎

{subfigure}

.2

Figure 6:
A graph
G_{\mu}
that is a parallel composition of seven components; the thin subgraph is show in gray. The component
G_{\nu_{j_{1}}}
(shown in orange) is a normal component for
u
and
v
with
\lambda(G_{\nu_{j_{1}}},v)=-1
; the component
G_{\nu_{j_{2}}}
(shown in green) is a special component of
u
.

In the next lemma, if
w\in\{u,v\}
is a top vertex then
\rho_{w}=out
, otherwise
\rho_{w}=in
.

Lemma 18
.

Let
\mu
be a
P
-node such that
G_{\mu}
is upward planar and let
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
be an upward planar embedding of
G_{\mu}
such that the left-turn-number of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
is
c
. Then the shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
is

[s_{1}\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},s_{2}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}},s_{3}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}}]
, with

s_{1}=\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,

s_{2}=\langle c-2,\allowbreak-c+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,
s_{3}=\langle c-4,\allowbreak-c+4,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
.

Proof.

Since all components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
are normal components of both poles
u
and
v
, it follows that
u
and
v
are switch vertices of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Hence, for every
i=1,\cdots,k^{\prime}
,
\rho_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},u)=\rho_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},u)=\rho_{u}
and
\rho_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},v)=\rho_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},v)=\rho_{v}
. Additionally, at each pole
u
and
v
there is exactly one large angle. By our assumptions
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},u)=\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},v)=1
for
i=1,\cdots,k^{\prime}
, hence the large angles at
u
and
v
lie inside two (not necessarily distinct) faces of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Let
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
be the two faces where the large angle at
u
and
v
lie in respectively, where
0\leq i,j<k^{\prime}
.
We distinguish some cases depending on whether
i=0
or
j=0
holds.

Figure 7:
Illustration for Lemma
18
. Components with shape description
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
are shaded with gray, orange and green respectively.

Case 1:

i=j=0
. The large angles at
u
and
v
are both inside the outer face of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
, that is,
\lambda(G^{\prime}_{\mu},u)=\lambda(G^{\prime}_{\mu},v)=1
(see Fig.
7
). In this case, by Lemma
9
, the left and the right turn number of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
are the same in absolute value, that is
\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u,v)=-\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u,v)
. Since the angles at
u
and
v
inside the face
f_{h}
is small, it follows from Lemma
10
that
\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)=-\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h+1}},u,v)
, for each
h=1,2,\dots,k-1
. Also, since the angles at
u
and
v
inside the internal faces of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
are small, it follows that
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u)=\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},v)=1
, which implies by Lemma
9
that
\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)=-\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)
. Combining these equalities we have that all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{h}}
(gray components in Fig.
7
) have the same shape description

\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,
where
c=\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u,v)
.
Hence the shape sequence is
[\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]
.

Case 2:

i=0
and
j>0
. The large angle at
u
is inside the outer face of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
while the large angle at
v
is inside face
f^{\prime}_{j}
, with
1\leq j<k^{\prime}
, that is,
\lambda(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u)=1
and
\lambda(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},v)=-1
(see Fig.
7
).
By Lemma
10
we have that
\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}},u,v)=-\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{i+1}},u,v)-2
.
For all
h\neq j
, the angles at
u
and
v
inside the face
f^{\prime}_{h}
are small, hence from Lemma
10
it follows that
\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)=-\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h+1}},u,v)
. Also, since the angles at
u
and
v
inside the internal faces of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
are small, it follows that
\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u)=\lambda(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},v)=1
, which implies by Lemma
9
that
\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)=-\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)
.
Combining these equations, we conclude that all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{h}}
with
h\leq j
(gray components in Fig.
7
) have the same shape description

\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,
where
c=\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u,v)
,
while all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu_{h}}
with
h>j
(orange components in Fig.
7
) have the same, yet different, shape description

\langle c-2,\allowbreak-c+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
.
Then the shape sequence for
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
is

[\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},\langle c-2,\allowbreak-c+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]

Case 3:

i>0
and
j=0
. The large angle at
v
is inside the outer face of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
while the large angle at
u
is inside face
f^{\prime}_{i}
, with
1\leq i<k^{\prime}
, that is,
\lambda(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u)=-1
and
\lambda(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},v)=1
(see Fig.
7
).
Symmetrically to the previous case, we can prove that all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
with
h\leq i
(gray components in Fig.
7
) have the same shape description

\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,
where
c=\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u,v)
, while all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
with
h>i
(orange components in Fig.
7
) have the same, yet different, shape description

\langle c-2,\allowbreak-c+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
.
As before, the shape sequence for
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
is

[\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},\langle c-2,\allowbreak-c+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]
.

Case 4:

i,j>0
. None of the large angles at
u
and
v
is inside the outer face of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu}
, that is,
\lambda(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u)=\lambda(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},v)=-1
. The large angle at
u
is inside face
f^{\prime}_{i}
, with
1\leq i<k^{\prime}
and the large angle at
v
is inside face
f^{\prime}_{j}
, with
1\leq j<k^{\prime}
.
Assume first that
i<j
holds. For each
h\neq i,j
, we have that the angles at
u
and
v
inside the face
f^{\prime}_{h}
and inside the internal faces of
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
are small. By Lemmata
10
and
9
it follows that
\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)=-\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h+1}},u,v)
and
\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)=-\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}},u,v)
. This means that all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
with
h\leq i
(gray components in Fig.
7
) have the same shape description

\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,
where
c=\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{\mu},u,v)
, all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
with
i<h\leq j
(orange components in Fig.
7
) have the same shape description

\langle c-2,\allowbreak-c+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,
and all
\mathcal{E}_{\nu^{\prime}_{h}}
with
j<h
(green components in Fig.
7
) have the same shape description

\langle c-4,\allowbreak-c+4,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
; see Fig.
7
.
The shape sequence defined is
[\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},\langle c-2,\allowbreak-c+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},\langle c-4,\allowbreak-c+4,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]
.
Note that the shape sequence is the same when
i>j
, while for
i=j
it becomes

[\langle c,\allowbreak-c,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},\langle c-4,\allowbreak-c+4,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]
; see Fig.
7
. ∎

Based on Lemma
18
, our algorithm computes the shape descriptions of all possible upward planar embeddings of
G_{\mu}
that match some fixed left- and right-turn-numbers in three steps. In the first step, we decide which sequences
S^{\prime}
of shape descriptions given in Lemma
18
are shape sequences for some maximal subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
of
G_{\mu}
. If
S^{\prime}
is such a sequence, then, by Lemma
17
, there are at most two children whose shape descriptions are not in
S^{\prime}
. So, in the second step we compute all possible extensions of
S^{\prime}
with the missing shape descriptions to shape sequences of
G_{\mu}
. In the last step, an extended sequence
S
of
G_{\mu}
is modified by appropriately changing the shape descriptions for at most two components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
in order to match the given left- and right-turn-numbers.

We now describe in detail how to compute the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of a P-node
\mu
. For each possible value
c_{l}
of
\tau_{l}(\mathcal{E}_{\mu},u,v)
and
c_{r}
of
\tau_{r}(\mathcal{E}_{\mu},u,v)
, that is
c_{l},c_{r}\in[\tau_{min},\tau_{max}]
, we compute the shape descriptions for all possible upward planar embeddings of
G_{\mu}
with the given left- and right-turn-numbers. All normal components of
G_{\mu}
have
uv
-external upward planar embeddings with left- and right-turn-number odd if both
u
and
v
are either top or bottom vertices, and even if one of them is a top vertex and the other one is a bottom vertex.
Then any
uv
-external upward planar embedding of every subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
that contains only normal components has a maximal left-turn-number
c^{\prime}_{l}
that is either equal to
c_{l}
or to
c_{l}-1
depending on the parity of both
c_{l}
and
c^{\prime}_{l}
. For the first step of the algorithm we consider all sequences of shape descriptions
S^{\prime}=[s_{1}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}},s_{2}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}},s_{3}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}}]
where
s_{1}=\langle c_{l}^{\prime},\allowbreak-c_{l}^{\prime},\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,

s_{2}=\langle c_{l}^{\prime}-2,\allowbreak-c_{l}^{\prime}+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
,
s_{3}=\langle c_{l}^{\prime}-4,\allowbreak-c_{l}^{\prime}+4,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
.
For each of them we identify a maximal subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
of
G_{\mu}
such that
S^{\prime}
is a shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. To this aim, we check whether it is possible to choose a shape description from the feasible set of the pertinent graph
G_{\nu_{i}}
of each child
\nu_{i}
of
\mu
(with
i=1,2,\dots,k
) and to sort these pertinent graphs so that the resulting shape sequence is
S^{\prime}
. We observe that
S^{\prime}
contains at most three different shape descriptions, namely
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
which can occur multiple times in the sequence. For each child
\nu_{i}
of
\mu
(with
i=1,2,\dots,k
) we check whether the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\nu_{i}}
contains one of the three shape descriptions of
S^{\prime}
in the order that they appear in
S^{\prime}
; if so, we choose it for
G_{\nu_{i}}
. Note that the shape description assigned to a child is the first one among
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
that is contained in its feasible set. However, this does not necessarily produce the desired sequence
S^{\prime}
for
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
, especially if
S^{\prime}
has two or three elements. Thus, we check whether it is possible to modify the assigned shape descriptions so as to produce the sequence
S^{\prime}
.
If
S^{\prime}
consists of two elements and all components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
are assigned to the first one, we check whether one of them can be reassigned the second one. If this is not possible, then no subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
has
S^{\prime}
as its shape sequence. If
S^{\prime}
consists of three elements, that is
S^{\prime}=[s_{1}\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},s_{2}\boldsymbol{{}^{+}},s_{3}\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]
, then we first make sure that
s_{2}
has at least one assigned component by reassigning a component from
s_{1}
, as in the previous case. If this is not possible, then no subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
has
S^{\prime}
as its shape sequence. Otherwise, if
s_{3}
has at least one assigned component we are done. If not, we check whether a component from
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
can be reassigned to
s_{3}
. If such a component exists, and both
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
have still at least one element, we are done. Otherwise if
s_{1}
is empty, then no subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
has
S^{\prime}
as its shape sequence; if
s_{2}
is empty we check whether a component from
s_{1}
can be reassigned to
s_{2}
.

For the second step of the algorithm, assume that for some sequence
S^{\prime}
we have identified a maximal subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
of
G_{\mu}
such that
S^{\prime}
is a shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Consider now those children of
\mu
where no shape description could be assigned for their pertinent graph. Recall that by Lemma
17
there can be at most two such children of
\mu
, say
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
. If this is not true, we conclude that
G_{\mu}
does not admit an upward drawing whose thin subgraph has
S^{\prime}
as its shape sequence.
Otherwise, let
s_{\nu}
(
s_{\nu^{\prime}}
) be a shape description in the feasible set of
\nu
(
\nu^{\prime}
, respectively), if it exists. Using Lemma
16
we compute all possible extensions of
S^{\prime}
with
s_{\nu}
to shape sequences of
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
, where
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}
is the subgraph of
G_{\mu}
consisting of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and
G_{\nu}
. If
\nu^{\prime}
does not exist, then
G^{\prime\prime}_{\mu}=G_{\mu}
and each computed shape sequence
S^{\prime\prime}
is a shape sequence of
G_{\mu}
. Otherwise, using the same lemma, for each computed shape sequence
S^{\prime\prime}
, we further compute all possible extensions of
S^{\prime\prime}
with
s_{\nu^{\prime}}
to shape sequences of
G_{\mu}
. For every computed shape sequence
S
of
G_{\mu}
we check whether it matches the given left- and right-turn-numbers, that is, the left-turn-number of the first element of
S
equals
c_{l}
and the right-turn-number of its last element equals
c_{r}
. If this is the case, then we add all shape descriptions for
G_{\mu}
that correspond to
S
to the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
. Otherwise, we proceed to the third step of the algorithm with
S
.

For the last step of our algorithm, let
S
be a shape sequence of
G_{\mu}
given by the previous step. That is,
S
is an extension of
S^{\prime}
and contains
s_{\nu}
and
s_{\nu^{\prime}}
for children
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
(if they exist) at specific positions, and such that the given left-turn-number and/or the right-turn-numbers are not matched. We consider cases depending on whether only one or both of the two turn-numbers are not matched.
Assume first the the first element of
S
has left-turn-number different from
c_{l}
, while the last element of
S
has right-turn-number equal to
c_{r}
. Then the first element has left-turn-number smaller than
c_{l}
.
Our goal is to find a component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
of the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
, remove its current shape description from
S
and use another one from its feasible set at the beginning of the sequence in order to match the desired left-turn-number. Note that this is possible only if
c_{l}
and
c^{\prime}_{l}
have the same parity.
So, consider component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
of the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and let
s_{i}\in\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}
be the shape description assigned to
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
in
S
. Let
s^{\prime}_{i}
be another shape description in the feasible set of
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
that is different from
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
. We denote by
S^{-}
the sequence
S
after removing
s_{i}
. Before proceeding, we want to assure that
S^{-}
contains the same elements with
S
. This is not true if
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
was the only component assigned to
s_{i}
during the first step of the algorithm. Note that all components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
are normal components for both
u
and
v
and the shape descriptions in their feasible sets have maximum left-turn-number equal to
c_{l}^{\prime}
. Hence
s_{1}
does not belong to
S
, that is, the shape sequence of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
is
S^{\prime}=[s_{2}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}},s_{3}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}}]
and
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
is initially assigned either to
s_{2}
or to
s_{3}
. If
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
was the only component of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
assigned to
s_{2}
, then
s_{2}
does not belong to the feasible set of the other components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and
S^{-}
can’t be modified to contain all elements of
S^{\prime}
. If
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
was the only component of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
assigned to
s_{3}
, then we check whether
s_{2}
belongs to the feasible set of the other components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. If we can’t find such a component, then
S^{-}
can’t be modified to contain all elements of
S^{\prime}
, otherwise, we reassign that component to
s_{2}
and
S^{-}
contains the same elements with
S
. Using Lemma
15
, we can compute the shape descriptions of all upward planar embeddings of
G_{\mu}
that correspond to the sequence
[s^{\prime}_{i},S^{-}]
. The computed shape descriptions have the desired left- and right-turn-number and are added to the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
.

For the case where
S
matches the left-turn-number
c_{l}
but the last element of
S
has right-turn-number different from
c_{r}
, we follow a similar approach. In this case,
s_{3}
does not belong to
S
and for each component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
of the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
we first remove its current shape description
s_{i}
from
S
. If the new sequence
S^{-}
does not contain all elements of
S^{\prime}
, then
s_{i}=s_{2}
and we check if we can reassign another component of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
from
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
. If this is not possible, then
S^{-}
can’t be modified to contain all elements of
S^{\prime}
. Otherwise, by Lemma
15
, we can compute all possible shape descriptions for
G_{\mu}
that correspond to the sequence
[S^{-},s^{\prime}_{i}]
. The computed shape descriptions match the desired left- and right-turn-number and are added to the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
.

In the special case where both the left- and right-turn-number of
S
do not match
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
respectively, then
S
does not contain neither
s_{1}
nor
s_{3}
. Hence, the initial sequence for the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
is
S^{\prime}=[s_{2}\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]
. Now, the shape sequence of a normal component for both
u
and
v
that has left-turn-number equal to
c^{\prime}_{l}
, right-turn-number at most
-c^{\prime}_{l}+2
and at least one label of the angles at
u
or
v
is
-1
, is either
t_{l}^{u}=\langle c^{\prime}_{l},\allowbreak-c^{\prime}_{l}+2,\allowbreak-1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
or
t_{l}^{v}=\langle c^{\prime}_{l},\allowbreak-c_{l}^{\prime}+2,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak-1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
(see Lemma
9
). Similarly the shape sequence of such a component with left-turn-number at most
c_{l}^{\prime}-2
and right-turn-number
-c_{l}^{\prime}+4
is either
t_{r}^{u}=\langle c^{\prime}_{l}-2,\allowbreak-c^{\prime}_{l}+4,\allowbreak-1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
or
t_{r}^{v}=\langle c^{\prime}_{l}-2,\allowbreak-c_{l}^{\prime}+4,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak-1,\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{u},\allowbreak\rho_{v},\allowbreak\rho_{v}\rangle
. In order to match both the given left- and right-turn-numbers,
S
needs to be extended with
t_{l}^{u}
or
t_{l}^{v}
at the beginning, and with
t_{r}^{u}
or
t_{r}^{v}
at the end. As
t_{l}^{u}
and
t_{r}^{u}
both have
\lambda(u)=-1
, and
t_{l}^{v}
and
t_{r}^{v}
both have
\lambda(v)=-1
, the possible extensions of
S
are
[t_{l}^{u},S,t_{r}^{v}]
and
[t_{l}^{v},S,t_{r}^{u}]
. We consider all components
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
of the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and check whether
t_{l}^{u}
,
t_{l}^{v}
,
t_{r}^{u}
and
t_{r}^{v}
are contained in their feasible set. Let
T_{l}^{u}
be the set of components
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
that contain
t_{l}^{u}
in their feasible set; sets
T_{l}^{v}
,
T_{r}^{u}
and
T_{r}^{v}
are defined similarly. For the sequence
[t_{l}^{u},S,t_{r}^{v}]
we want to select one component from
T_{l}^{u}
and a different one from
T_{r}^{v}
and reassign their shape descriptions from
s_{2}
to
t_{l}^{u}
and
t_{r}^{v}
respectively. This is not possible only if either one of
T_{l}^{u}
or
T_{r}^{v}
is empty, or if
T_{l}^{u}
is the same as
T_{r}^{v}
and they both contain one element. If this is the case,
S
can’t be extended to
[t_{l}^{u},S,t_{r}^{v}]
, otherwise, we use Lemma
15
to decide whether this is a shape sequence of
G_{\mu}
and to compute the corresponding shape descriptions for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
. Similarly,
S
can’t be extended to the second sequence
[t_{l}^{v},S,t_{r}^{u}]
if either one of
T_{l}^{v}
and
T_{r}^{u}
is empty or if they both have size one and contain the same element, otherwise the shape descriptions for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
that correspond to
[t_{l}^{v},S,t_{r}^{u}]
are computed with Lemma
15
and added to the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
.

Lemma 19
.

Let
\mu
be an P-node of
T
. The described algorithm computes correctly the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
.

Proof.

Our algorithm computes sequences of shape descriptions for
G_{\mu}
based on the feasible sets of the pertinent graphs of the children of
\mu
. For each computed sequence Lemma
15
tests whether it is a shape sequence of
G_{\mu}
and the corresponding shape descriptions are added in the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
. Hence, whenever a shape description
s
is computed for a sequence
S
, there exists an upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
of
G_{\mu}
with shape sequence
S
and whose shape description is
s
.

It remains to prove that if
s_{\mu}
is a shape description of
G_{\mu}
, then our algorithm finds a shape sequence
S
such that
s_{\mu}
corresponds to
S
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
be an upward planar embedding of
G_{\mu}
with shape sequence
S_{\mu}
and such that the shape description of
G_{\mu}
is
s_{\mu}
. Let
G^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mu}}
be the thin subgraph of
G_{\mu}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
and let
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
be its shape sequence.

For the left- and right-turn-numbers of
s_{\mu}
, the first step of the algorithm computes a maximal thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
for
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
assigning each child of
G_{\mu}
to the first element of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
that is contained in its feasible set. If an element of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
is not assigned, the algorithm tries to reassign some components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. This is not possible only if an element of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
is not contained in the feasible sets of all components of
G_{\mu}
. Note that the children of
\mu
whose pertinent graphs belong to the thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mu}}
contain at least one element of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
in their feasible set. Hence, the algorithm will successfully compute a maximal thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
for
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Additionally, the computed maximal thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
contains all components of
G^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mu}}
, although they may be assigned to different elements of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
.

If
G^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mu}}=G_{\mu}
, that is
S_{\mu}=S^{\prime}_{\mu}
, then all components of
G_{\mu}
are assigned to elements of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
, the second step can’t extend
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
, and the left- and right-turn-numbers of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
match the given values. Hence, during the third step of the algorithm all shape descriptions for
G_{\mu}
that correspond to
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
will be computed, including
s_{\mu}
. So, assume that there is at least one component of
G_{\mu}
, say
G_{\nu}
, whose shape description
s_{\nu}
in
S_{\mu}
is not an element of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
. If
G_{\nu}
is a special component of one of the poles, or a normal component for both poles whose feasible set contains no element of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
, then the second step of the algorithm tests whether
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
can be extended with all shape descriptions in the feasible set of
G_{\nu}
, including
s_{\nu}
. Since
S_{\mu}
is a shape sequence of
G_{\mu}
, the algorithm will extend
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
with
s_{\nu}
to all possible shape sequences of the subgraph consisting of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
and
G_{\nu}
. In particular the sequence where
s_{\nu}
is placed between the same elements of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
as in
S_{\mu}
, will be one of the computed shape sequences. If there is another component
G_{\nu^{\prime}}
that is either a special component of one of the poles, or a normal component for both poles whose feasible set contains no element of
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
, then
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
will be further extended to contain the shape description
s_{\nu^{\prime}}
of
G_{\nu^{\prime}}
at the correct position. At the end of the second step, either the computed shape sequence
S
is the same with
S_{\mu}
, or all elements of
S
appear in
S_{\mu}
in the same order from left to right.

If
S
has the same left- and right-turn-numbers with
S_{\mu}
, then the set of shape descriptions that correspond to
S
will include
s_{\mu}
, since all elements of
S
are contained in
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
. So, we examine the case where the left- and/or the right-turn-numbers of
S
do not match those of
S_{\mu}
. Assume first that the left-turn-number is different from
S_{\mu}
, while the right-turn-number is the same. Let
s_{1}
be the first element of
S_{\mu}
that corresponds to component
G_{\nu_{1}}
of
G_{\mu}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
. Then,
G_{\nu_{1}}
is a component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
of the maximal thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
computed in the first step, and its shape description in
S
belongs to
S^{\prime}_{\mu}
. During the third step of the algorithm, it will be tested whether the sequence
[s_{1},S]
is a shape sequence for
G_{\mu}
. Note that since all elements of
[s_{1},S]
belong to
S_{\mu}
in the same order, and the left- and right-turn-numbers of the two sequences are the same, the shape description
s_{\mu}
of
G_{\mu}
corresponds to both
S_{\mu}
and
[s_{1},S]
and will be therefore computed by the algorithm. Similarly, if
S_{\mu}
and
S
have the same left-turn-number and different right-turn-number, the third step of the algorithm will identify
s_{\mu}
as a shape description corresponding to the sequence
[S,s_{k}]
, where
s_{k}
is the last element of
S_{\mu}
and it is the shape description used for the last component
G_{\nu_{k}}
of
G_{\mu}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
. Hence it remains to consider the case where
S
and
S_{\mu}
have different left- and right-turn-numbers. In this case
S_{\mu}
is one of
[t_{l}^{u},S^{\prime}_{\mu},t_{r}^{v}]
or
[t_{l}^{v},S^{\prime}_{\mu},t_{r}^{u}]
, and
S^{\prime}_{\mu}=[s_{2}\boldsymbol{{}^{+}}]
. The last part of the algorithm tests whether it is possible to reassign two components of
G_{\mu}
from
s_{2}
, one to
t_{l}^{u}
and the other to
t_{r}^{v}
for the first sequence, or one to
t_{l}^{v}
and the other to
t_{r}^{u}
for the second one. Hence
S_{\mu}
will be successfully identified as a shape sequence of
G_{\mu}
, concluding the lemma.
∎

Lemma 20
.

Let
\mu
be an P-node of
T
. The feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(\tau\cdot k)
time, where
k
is the number of children of
\mu
.

Proof.

We prove that the time complexity of the algorithm described above is
\mathcal{O}(\tau\cdot k)
.
The number of choices for the left-turn-number
c_{l}
of a
uv
-external upward planar embedding of
G_{\mu}
is
\tau
, and for each value of
c_{l}
by Lemma
9
the right-turn-number
c_{r}
can only take values
-c_{l}+h
for
h=0,1,2,3,4
. Hence there are
5\tau
possible pairs for
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
. For each pair,
we compute the shape descriptions of all possible
uv
-external upward planar embeddings of
G_{\mu}
with left-turn-number equal to
c_{l}
and right-turn-number equal to
c_{r}
. For given
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
, first we retrieve the feasible sets of the children of
\mu
. For each feasible set we are only interested in those shape descriptions that have left-turn-number in
[c_{l}-4,c_{l}]
and right-turn-number in
[c_{r}-4,c_{r}]
. Let
|\mathcal{F}|
denote the size of the largest feasible set under these constraints. By Lemma
12
the shape descriptions with specific left- and right-turn-numbers are at most six and can be fetched in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time. Hence
|\mathcal{F}|
is a constant and all feasible sets can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(k|\mathcal{F}|)
time.

For the first step of the algorithm, we consider all possible sequences
S^{\prime}=[s_{1}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}},s_{2}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}},s_{3}\boldsymbol{{}^{*}}]
. There are eight such sequences depending on whether each of
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
belongs to the sequence or not. Let
S^{\prime}
be one of them.
For
i=1,\ldots,k
we check if
\mathcal{F}_{\nu_{i}}
contains one of the at most three elements of
S^{\prime}
; by Lemma
12
each check takes
\mathcal{O}(1)
time, and we store which nodes among
\nu_{i}
are assigned to
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
and
s_{3}
(if they belong to
S^{\prime}
). This step takes
\mathcal{O}(k)
time and the maximal thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
is identified. If an element of
S^{\prime}
has no nodes assigned to it, at most two components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
are reassigned. Each reassignment process takes
\mathcal{O}(k)
time, since a specific shape description is looked for in the feasible sets of the components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Hence the maximal thin subgraph
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
with shape sequence
S^{\prime}
is identified in
\mathcal{O}(k)
time or the algorithm concludes that there is no subgraph of
G_{\mu}
with shape sequence
S^{\prime}
.

The second step considers children
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
of
\mu
(if they exist). For each
s_{\nu}\in\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
and
s_{\nu^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\nu^{\prime}}
, shape sequence
S^{\prime}
is extended in all possible ways with
s_{\nu}
and
s_{\nu^{\prime}}
to a shape sequence
S
for
G_{\mu}
. Each extension uses Lemma
16
and requires
\mathcal{O}(1)
time, since
S^{\prime}
has size at most three. Hence, computing all possible sequences
S
for
G_{\mu}
that extend a specific shape sequence
S^{\prime}
of the first step can be done in
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{F}|^{2})
time.

Checking whether the left- or right-turn-number of a sequence is equal to a given number takes
\mathcal{O}(1)
time. If both the left- and right-turn-numbers are matched by
S
, the shape descriptions that correspond to
S
are computed with Lemma
15
in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time (as
S
has size at most five). Otherwise the algorithm proceeds with the last step. We consider two cases depending on whether one of
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
are not matched or both. Consider first the case where one of them is not matched. Assume that the first element of
S
has left-turn-number different from
c_{l}
and the right-turn-number equals
c_{r}
; the case where
S
matches
c_{l}
and not
c_{r}
is similar. There are at most
k
choices for selecting component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
. Computing the sequence
S^{-}
takes constant time since its size is at most five. For at most one component, say
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{j}}
,
S^{-}
might not contain all elements of
S^{\prime}
; for this component only, we reassign in
(k^{\prime}-1)\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{F}|)
time another component from
s_{2}
to
s_{3}
. We check whether all elements of
S^{\prime}
have at least one node assigned to them in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time. If this is the case, then the new sequence
S^{-}
contains all elements of
S^{\prime}
. For each shape description
s^{\prime}_{i}
of the feasible set of
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
with left-turn-number equal to
c_{l}
we compute all possible shape descriptions for
G_{\mu}
that correspond to the sequence
[s^{\prime}_{i},S^{-}]
in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time by Lemma
15
. As the computed shape descriptions match the desired left- and right-turn-number, they are added to the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time each. The process for a component
G_{\nu^{\prime}_{i}}
requires
\mathcal{O}(k|\mathcal{F}|^{2})
time if the sequence
S^{-}
does not contain all elements of
S^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{F}|)
time otherwise. Since this can occur for at most one component of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
, the third step runs in
\mathcal{O}(k|\mathcal{F}|^{2})
time, for the case where one of
c_{l}
or
c_{r}
are not matched.

In the special case where both the left- and right-turn-number of
S
do not match
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
respectively, then the four sets
T_{l}^{u}
,
T_{l}^{v}
,
T_{r}^{u}
and
T_{r}^{v}
are computed, by checking whether
t_{l}^{u}
,
t_{l}^{v}
,
t_{r}^{u}
or
t_{r}^{v}
respectively belong to the feasible set of all components of
G^{\prime}_{\mu}
. By Lemma
12
each check takes
\mathcal{O}(1)
time, hence the sets are computed in
\mathcal{O}(k)
time. There are two possible sequences for
G_{\mu}
, namely
[t_{l}^{u},S,t_{r}^{v}]
and
[t_{l}^{v},S,t_{r}^{u}]
. For each of them, we check if any of the corresponding sets is empty, or if they have both size one and contain the same element. If this is the case
S
can’t be extended to match the given left- and right-turn-numbers, otherwise, by Lemma
15
the corresponding shape descriptions for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time; each shape description is added to
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
in
\mathcal{O}(1)
time. Hence, if
S
matches none of
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
the last step of the algorithm requires
\mathcal{O}(k)
time.

The overall time complexity is
5\tau(\mathcal{O}(k|\mathcal{F}|)+8\cdot(\mathcal{O}(k)+\mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{F}|^{2})\cdot(\mathcal{O}(k|\mathcal{F}|^{2})+\mathcal{O}(k))))
, where
|\mathcal{F}|=\mathcal{O}(1)
, that is
\mathcal{O}(\tau\cdot k)
, as claimed.
∎

R-node

While for S-, P- and Q-nodes we were able to provide polynomial-time procedures to compute the feasible sets, the
\mathrm{NP}
-hardness of
Upward Planarity
prevents the existence of such a polynomial-time procedure for R-nodes. Indeed, obtaining a suitable algorithm that can compute the feasible sets for an R-node given the feasible sets for all of its children will be the task of our parameterized algorithms presented in Sections
6
and
7
. In order to complete the description of our framework and also present it in a modular fashion which can be reused by future work, here we formalize the notion of an
R-node subprocedure
which, intuitively, is an algorithm that can compute a feasible set of an R-node.

Formally, an
R-node subprocedure
is an algorithm which takes as input an
R
-node
\mu
of
T
with skeleton
H
, a mapping
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}
which assigns each child of
\mu
to its feasible set, runs in time at most
\alpha(G_{\mu},\mathcal{S}_{\mu})
, and computes the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
. For a digraph
G
with SPQR-tree
T
, let
\alpha(G)
be
\sum\limits_{\text{R-node }\mu}\alpha(G_{\mu},\mathcal{S}_{\mu})
. We say that
\alpha(G)
is the
total time complexity
of the R-node subprocedure for graph
G
.

Root node

We now focus on the root node of the SPQR-tree
T
. The root node
r
corresponds to an edge
e=(u,v)
of
G
that lies on its outer face. Node
r
has only one child node
\mu
with poles
u
and
v
. Let
s
be a shape description in the feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\mu}
of
\mu
. Then, there exists a
uv
-external upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
of the pertinent graph
G_{\mu}
of node
\mu
, such that
G_{\mu}
has shape description
s
, and poles
u
and
v
are on the outer face of
G_{\mu}
. Note that
G_{\mu}=G\setminus e
. Hence,
r
can be treated as a P-node with poles
u
and
v
and two children; one of them is
\mu
and the other one is a Q-node, say
\mu^{\prime}
for the edge
(u,v)
. Then the feasible set of
\mu^{\prime}
contains only one shape description, namely
s^{\prime}=\langle 0,\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak{out},\allowbreak{out},\allowbreak{in},\allowbreak{in}\rangle
. By Lemma
20
we can compute in
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
time the feasible set of
r
. Since
r
has only two children, any shape sequence
S
of
r
contains
s^{\prime}
as its first or last element. This implies that for any upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}
of
G_{r}
with shape sequence
S
, either the left or the right outer path of
G_{\mu^{\prime}}
belongs to the outer face of
G_{r}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}
. As
G_{\mu^{\prime}}
consists only of edge
(u,v)
, it follows that
(u,v)
belongs to the outer face of
G
in all possible upward planar embeddings.

Lemma 21
.

The feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{r}
of the root node
r
of
T
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(\tau)
time.

Time complexity

From the discussion above and in particular from Lemmata
13
,
14
,
20
and
21
, we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 22
.

Let
G
be a biconnected DAG with
n
vertices and let
T
be an SPQR-tree of
G
rooted at a
Q
-node corresponding to an edge
e=(u,v)
. Let
\tau_{\min}
and
\tau_{\max}
be two given integer values, and let
\tau=\tau_{\max}-\tau_{\min}+1
. Given an R-node subprocedure with total time complexity
\alpha(G)
, it is possible to compute in time
\mathcal{O}(\alpha(G)+\tau^{2}\cdot n)
the shape descriptions of every upward planar embedding with
e
on the outer face, such that the left- and right-turn-numbers of the pertinent graph of every node of
T
are in the range
[\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}]
.

5
Extension to the Single-Connected Case

In this section, we show that Lemma
22
can be used as a blackbox to extend our results from the biconnected case to arbitrary graphs.
Our goal is to prove Interface Lemma
24
, which reduces the task of solving
Upward Planarity
to the one of obtaining an R-node subprocedure, for
all
graphs including single-connected ones.
First, in the next definition we encapsulate the property that the shapes occuring in SPQR-tree decompositions of a graph have a bounded range of left- and right-turn-numbers, as in Sections
6
and
7
we obtain efficient algorithms precisely in this regime.

Definition 23
.

Let
G
be a digraph and
\tau_{\min}
,
\tau_{\max}
be two integers. We call
G

[\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}]
-turn-bounded
if the following holds for every upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}
of
G
. For any biconnected component
B
of
G
and every SPQR-tree decomposition
T
of
B
that is rooted at a Q-node corresponding to an edge that lies on the outer face of
B
in the embedding induced by
\mathcal{E}
, left- and right-turn-numbers of the pertinent graph of every node of
T
are in range
[\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}]
.

We now give the precise statement of our Interface Lemma.

Lemma 24

(Interface Lemma)
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex digraph, and
\tau_{\min}
,
\tau_{\max}
be integers such that
G
is
[\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}]
-turn-bounded.
Given an R-node subprocedure with total time complexity
\alpha(G)
, it is possible to determine whether
G
admits an upward planar embedding in time
\mathcal{O}(n(\alpha(G)+\tau^{2}\cdot n))
where
\tau=\tau_{\max}-\tau_{\min}+1
.

Note that, for a single-connected graph
G
, we define the total time complexity
\alpha(G)
of an R-node subprocedure to be the sum of
\alpha(B)
over all biconnected components
B
of
G
.

To give an intuition of the proof, we start by guessing the root of the block-cut tree of
G
, which is assumed to see the outer face in the desired upward planar embedding of
G
.
The core of the proof is the following lemma, which states that leaf components can always be added on top of an upward planar embedding, up to simple conditions on the cut-vertex.

Lemma 25
.

Consider a rooted block-cut tree of G, its cut vertex
v
that is adjacent to leaf blocks
B_{1}
,…,
B_{\ell}
, and the parent block
P
. Denote by
G^{P}
the corresponding component of
G-\{v\}
.
Any upward planar embedding of
G^{P}
where the root block sees the outer face can be extended to an embedding of
G
with the same property if the following holds:

1.

Each
B_{i}
has an upward-planar embedding with
v
on the outer face.

2.

If
v
is a non-switch vertex in
P
, each
B_{i}
has an upward-planar embedding with
v
on the outer face where the large angle of
v
is on the outer face.

As the conditions of Lemma
25
turn out to be necessary as well, our algorithm simply proceeds upwards along the block-cut tree, checking every time the conditions of the lemma and removing the respective leaf components. If at some point the conditions are not satisfied, we conclude that
G
is a no-instance; otherwise, any upward planar embedding of the root block can be iteratively extended to an embedding of the whole graph. Finally, finding upward planar embeddings of the blocks is performed via Lemma
22
; it can also be used to verify conditions of Lemma
25
.

Proof of Lemma
25
.

Fix an upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}^{P}
of
G^{P}
, and upward plannar embeddings
\mathcal{E}^{B_{1}}
, …,
\mathcal{E}^{B_{\ell}}
of
B_{1}
, …,
B_{\ell}
subject to the conditions of the lemma.
W.l.o.g. assume that the leaf blocks are indexed in such a way that for the first
p
blocks, the angle of
v
at the outer face is small or flat, for some
0\leq p\leq\ell
, and for the remaining blocks the outer angle at
v
is large. If
p>0
, it follows from the conditions of the lemma that
v
is a switch vertex in
P
and in all the blocks
B_{1}
, …,
B_{\ell}
except maybe one; if there is a block where
v
is a non-switch vertex w.l.o.g. let that block be
B_{p}
.

We first consequently deal with the blocks
B_{1}
, …,
B_{p}
, that is, we construct an upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}_{i}
of
G_{i}=G[V(G_{p})\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}V(B_{j})]
for each
i\in[p]
from an upward planar embedding
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
of
G_{i-1}
, starting with the embedding
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathcal{E}^{P}
of
G_{0}=G^{P}
. For each vertex of
G_{i}
that is not
v
, we infer both the circular order of the incident edges and the angle assignment from
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
or
\mathcal{E}^{B_{i}}
, depending on whether the vertex belongs to
B_{i}
or not. To define a circular order on edges incident to
v
, split the ordering on neighbors of
v
in
G_{i-1}
at a large angle of
v
(according to
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
), which necessarily exists as
v
is a switch vertex of
G_{i-1}
, and join it with the ordering of neighbors of
v
in
B_{i}
split at the outer angle of
v
in
\mathcal{E}^{B_{i}}
. This results in two new angles, for each of them, we assign a value of
-1
if it is a switch angle, and
0
if it is a flat angle. All the other angles around
v
keep their value from either
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
or
\mathcal{E}^{B_{i}}
. We now verify that the conditions
UP0
–
UP3
from Theorem
1
hold for the vertex
v
and the new face of the constructed embedding.
UP0
–
UP2
hold by construction,
next we verify
UP3
by considering two cases. In what follows,
f
denotes the newly formed face. We assume that
UP3
is already verified for
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
.

Case 1.
The large angle at
v
is inside the outer face of
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
. This necessarily implies
i=1
, as each
B_{i}
for
i\in[p]
is embedded with a small angle at
v
on the outer face. Since we embed
B_{1}
in the outer angle of
v
, the newly formed face is the outer face. Observe that the set of angles at
f
is precisely the union of the angles of
G_{0}
and the angles of
B_{1}
on the outer faces in the respective embeddings, except for the angles at
v
. The angle at
v
on the outer face is necessarily
1
in
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, and there are two cases for the value in
\mathcal{E}^{B_{1}}
. If the corresponding angle is a switch angle, then the value is
-1
, and the two newly formed angles at
v
in
f
by construction receive the value
-1
(see Fig.
8
). If the angle is flat in
B_{1}
, its value is
0
and one of the newly formed angles is also flat, receiving a value of
0
, while the other is a switch angle, receiving a value of
-1
(see Fig.
8
). In either case, the sum of angle values on
f
at
v
is two less than the sum of two outer angles at
v
in
G_{0}
and
B_{1}
.
Thus, summing up all angle values along
f
we obtain two (sum of angles on the outer face of
G_{0}
) plus two (sum of angles on the outer face of
B_{1}
minus two (difference in the angles at
v
), which satisfies
UP3
since
f
is the outer face.

Case 2.
The large angle at
v
is inside an inner face of
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
. In this case,
f
is also an inner face in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
. Analogously to Case 1, summing up the angles on
f
in
G_{i}
is equivalent to summing angles on the corresponding faces in
G_{i-1}
and
B_{i}
accounting for the change of angles at
v
. By the same arguments, the change at
v
results in minus two, while the sum in
G_{i-1}
is minus two (inner face) and the sum in
B_{i}
is two (outer face) by
UP3
in
\mathcal{E}_{i-1}
and
\mathcal{E}^{B_{i}}
, respectively. This sums up to minus two, fulfilling
UP3
for
f
in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

{subfigure}

.18
